societally optimal expansion of bicycle networks
mads paulsen ∗, jeppe rich
transport division, department of technology, management and economics, technical university of denmark, bygningstorvet 116b, 2800 kgs.
lyngby, denmark
a r t i c l e i n f o
keywords:
bicycle infrastructure
bicycle network design
benefit–cost analysis
transport network expansion
transport geography
cycle superhighways
a b s t r a c t
in this paper, we consider the problem of expanding bicycle networks over time. the expansion
of the network at a given point in time is based on the societal cost–benefit performance, which
entails system-wide effects from previous expansions. the problem is challenging due to non-
linearities of travel time benefits and the dimension of the problem, which is a consequence
of the combinatorial complexity of the networks and the planning horizon. it rules out the
use of conventional bottom-up cost–benefit analysis as evaluating even a small set of possible
solutions becomes computationally infeasible. to circumvent this problem, we introduce a novel
reverse geographical mapping approach where the monetary benefits are assigned back onto
the network. this allows a more detailed geographical planning breakdown at the level of
network links and makes it possible to apply a more stringent optimization approach with
respect to the timing and prioritization of network expansions. based on a linear approximation
of travel time savings, we propose several variants of mathematical integer programs to solve
the problem. this allows us to consider the case of growing a cycle superhighway network
in the copenhagen region over a time horizon of 50 years. we show that our approximations
of travel time savings are largely similar to those obtained through actual traffic assignment.
furthermore, the optimization approach renders a development plan, which yields a net present
value that is ten times larger than that of the actual infrastructure upgrades implemented since
2019. in a long-term scenario, it is shown that our solution returns an accumulated benefit–cost
ratio of 2.7 over the period, which is a significant improvement over previous findings. this
underlines the importance of optimal prioritization schemes of where and when to invest in
bicycle network expansions.
1. introduction
the geographical expansion of bicycle networks is a problem faced by most cities around the world, and it is becoming
increasingly relevant due to increased urbanization and raised environmental concern (buehler and pucher, 2021). from a technical
point of view, it is a planning problem, which, based on an existing bicycle network in year 𝑡 = 0, aims at providing support for a
range of planning decisions regarding the further expansion of the network. more specifically; (i) where should investments start?,
(ii) where should network investments be directed next (in 𝑡 = 1,𝑡 = 2, …)?, (iii) how to limit investments due to budget constraints
in specific time periods? and finally, (iv) when should investments stop?
the problem of expanding bicycle networks is a complex problem previously visited in the literature by e.g. mesbah et al. (2012),
smith and haghani (2012), duthie and unnikrishnan ( 2014), liu et al. ( 2019), guillermo et al. ( 2020), ospina et al. ( 2022). the
complexity arises from intricate dependencies between network segments, a planning horizon that takes place over many years, and

fig. 1. illustration of the bicycle network expansion problem. gray links represent the ‘gross network’ of links that can be expanded, black links represent the
network in the year 𝑡 = 0, whereas the remaining colors indicate expansions for the respective periods. the size of the nodes indicates the population size in
each node. (for interpretation of the references to color in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the web version of this article.)
the fact that early network expansions are taken into use before the completion of the entire expansion. a stylized geographical
illustration of the problem is presented in fig. 1 where a toy network is gradually expanded from a base year ( 𝑡 = 0 ) over three
time periods (𝑡 = 1,𝑡 = 2,𝑡 = 3).
although the figure is stylized, it is included as a means to convey important information regarding the problem at hand. firstly,
that it is often helpful to identify a relevant ‘gross-network’ which is only a small subset of all possible combinations of links between
nodes (mesbah et al., 2012; liu et al. , 2019). this reduces the solution space and eliminate irrelevant links. secondly, population
densities affect the development of the network ( mahfouz et al., 2021). some links are more important than others simply because
they connect larger population areas and thereby potentially will carry higher bicycle flows. thirdly, that there is a cost-side to
network expansions. longer links will be more expensive than shorter ones. fourthly, that increasing connectivity by connecting
sub-networks early may be a better strategy (schoner and levinson, 2014; guillermo et al., 2020; ryan et al., 2020) than developing
links in the outskirts of the network, although some studies argue for the opposite (e.g. duthie and unnikrishnan , 2014).
although traditional transport model projections are valuable when extending bicycle networks, they cannot determine exactly
when and where to invest in expansions. therefore, there is a need to rethink if and how welfare benefits of transport network
expansions can be expressed at more detailed levels than commonly employed, and to investigate if and how such ability can lead
to a more stringent optimization of transport network expansions. this is no easy task, as the different networks expansions are
often mutually dependent. a given network expansion often requires other expansions to be developed before the full benefits are
realized. however, it may also be that a competing parallel expansion has already been build, in which case the two projects will
cannibalize benefits from each other and render so-called diminishing returns of transport investments ( rodrigue and notteboom,
2020).
we circumvent this problem by drawing inspiration from common cost–benefit approaches, where project benefits are monetized
and compared to investments and maintenance costs before being evaluated at an overall level through performance indicators
such as net present value (npv) terms or benefit–cost ratios (bcr) (e.g. leleur , 2000), see appendix b.1 for formal definitions.
summarizing the various effects into a single monetary value reduces the complexity of the problem, and is the most commonly
used methodology when appraising large transport infrastructure projects ( browne and ryan , 2011; beukers et al. , 2012; mackie
et al., 2014). however, much information is lost during bottom-up aggregation from detailed route and zone-based data to overall
performance indicators ( beukers et al. , 2012). instead, we propose to assign the od-specific benefits back onto the network and
utilize the accumulated benefit flowing through each link to establish an approximation of the added benefit of each link.
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778
3
m. paulsen and j. rich
the paper provides three main contributions to the literature. firstly, we propose that monetary consumer benefits of any kind
can be distributed back onto the transport network by applying a traffic assignment model to the monetary benefits rather than
flows. the second contribution lies in the formulation of a stage-based cost–benefit optimization framework for transport network
expansions, that utilizes the resulting link-based measures of benefits. the key contribution is the formulation of a variant of the
mathematical problem that provides a good approximation while also being feasible for large-scale instances. the third contribution
is that we apply the optimization framework in a large-scale planning context for cycle superhighway expansions in a network of
copenhagen consisting of 471,266 links, 52,742 origin–destination pairs, and nine cyclist types. the resulting approach, we believe,
supports a new geographical perspective with respect to the distribution of benefits–cost performance at the level of links, that
allows applying standard binary linear optimization techniques to establish systematic prioritization schemes for a project portfolio
consisting of multiple potential transport network expansions. the importance of this work is underlined by demonstrating how the
proposed solutions lead to significant increases in the accumulated user benefits over time when compared to current practice and
basic ad hoc reference strategies.
the remainder of the paper is structured as follows. section 2 reviews the existing literature on bicycle infrastructure cost benefit
analyses and network design. in section 3 we formulate the mathematical problem and propose a linear formulation that can be
easily solved even for large problems. section 4 presents the results of a dynamic expansion of the cycle superhighway network
of greater copenhagen and compares how the societal performances differ when different strategies are applied. in addition, we
assess the validity of our linear approximation of travel time savings. in section 5 we offer a discussion and outline ideas for future
research activities, before concluding in section 6. all notation used in the paper can be found in appendix a.
2. literature review
the impact of bicycle infrastructure on bicycle demand is well documented (e.g. krizek et al., 2009; van goeverden and
godefrooij, 2011; van goeverden et al., 2015; zahabi et al., 2016; standen et al., 2017). increased demand often results from a
mixture of elements from increased speed (schleinitz et al., 2017; eriksson et al., 2019) to improved traffic safety (krizek et al.,
2009; van goeverden and godefrooij, 2011; van goeverden et al., 2015) and better cycling environments (zahabi et al., 2016;
standen et al., 2017), some of which have also been found important in the route choice cycling literature for which a review can
be found in pritchard (2018).
in a danish context, the danish office for cycle superhighways has evaluated each of the eight existing cycle superhighway routes
after their opening (sekretariatet for supercykelstier, 2019). they found that the number of cyclists along the routes increased from
2%–68% within the first year, and that the self-reported average length of the trips along the routes was 11 km, far exceeding
the average trip distances in the area as well as globally (banerjee et al., 2021). a specific route, ‘‘albertslundsruten’’, was studied
in skov-petersen et al. (2017). the results showed a significant increase in the number of bicycle trips of up to 61% for certain
periods of the year.
while there is evidence that infrastructure affects demand in many different ways, only few studies examine the combined
benefit–cost performance of cycle superhighway infrastructure. in denmark, the only recent relevant studies are hallberg et al.
(2021) and rich et al. (2021) who examined the cost–benefit of a large-scale cycle superhighway infrastructure. a previous more
limited study on the same network is due to incentive (2018). the findings in rich et al. (2021) suggested that the internal rate of
return on investment were between 8%–23%, and thereby rendered higher relative benefits compared to most other infrastructure
investments. the paper also highlighted that most of the benefits were associated to health benefits of which it was shown that
electric and conventional bicycles contributed differently. in all three cases, a static comparative evaluation approach was used.
in addition to the danish studies already mentioned krizek (2007) and brown et al. (2016) review the literature on bicycle
infrastructure cost benefit analysis. brown et al. (2016) consider 36 studies that include health effects and identify four studies that
do not. although it can be argued that health benefits are partly internalized (börjesson and eliasson, 2012), health benefits are
indeed of particular importance for bicycle infrastructure appraisal due to reductions in mortality (kelly et al., 2014) and reduced
health care costs as documented in the literature (e.g. de hartog et al., 2010; oja et al., 2011; brown et al., 2016; foley et al., 2018,
2019). several recent studies (standen et al., 2019; rich et al., 2021; fosgerau et al., 2023) have found that the consumer surplus
is a considerable contributor to the total benefit, roughly about 3–5 times lower than the health benefits. regarding the impact on
travel times for other road users, studies that take this into account generally consider travel time reductions as a consequence of
decreased car use (e.g. cope et al., 2010; rabl and de nazelle, 2012; li and faghri, 2014). one exception is rich et al. (2021) who
include negative effects resulting from reduced capacity. however, the effect is entirely based on external sources (incentive, 2018)
and the numbers are highly uncertain. the congestion disbenefits are found to be 30 times lower than the health benefits. although,
environmental factors are the second-most frequently assessed effect when appraising bicycle infrastructure according to brown et al.
(2016), they often constitute only a small part of the total benefits. the studies by guo and gandavarapu (2010), sælensminde
(2004), macmillan et al. (2014), rabl and de nazelle (2012), li and faghri (2014), and chapman et al. (2018), respectively,
find the effect to be 11, 12, 13, 45, 60, and 70 times lower than the health benefits. the third most frequently studied effect
are accidents/injuries (brown et al., 2016) which are sometimes evaluated as net benefits due to safer bicycle infrastructure (li
and faghri, 2014; macmillan et al., 2014), but sometimes evaluated as net costs due to increased accident risk when the bicycle
mileage increases (rabl and de nazelle, 2012; rich et al., 2021). other less common factors mentioned in brown et al. (2016)
include productivity, comfort/security, vehicle operating, and parking.
the current paper is not only connected to the bicycle demand and cost–benefit literature as described above, but also goes a
step further in exploring how bicycle networks should be grown over time from an efficiency point of view. this relates to other
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778
4
m. paulsen and j. rich
research contributions that are concerned with network design as described in recent papers by coscia and szell (2020), schläpfer
et al. (2021) and by guillermo et al. (2020) for the bicycle network design in particular. guillermo et al. (2020) develop different
greedy algorithms to increase the network connectivity and consider how the developed network compare to the real networks of
different cities. while this analysis is indeed useful, it is based on a pure graph theoretical approach and does not – as opposed to
the methodology proposed in this paper – consider the number of people affected by the expansion nor the benefits it may result
in. lovelace et al. (2020) and mahfouz et al. (2021) go a step further in their study of road segment prioritization for bicycle
infrastructure in a number of english cities, where potential bicycle demand between od pairs is estimated based on distance. the
prioritization is carried out using simple heuristics. the assessment is not anchored to cost–benefit analysis, but instead related to
network specific key performance indicators related to connectivity and trip shares covered by bicycle paths.
other papers have looked at the bicycle network design problem from operations research (or) perspectives. several approaches
have been considered. duthie and unnikrishnan (2014) set out to minimize investment costs whilst ensuring a certain level of service
for bicyclists. lower bounds of level of service are also ensured in lim et al. (2021), who instead minimize total detours.
recently, both chan et al. (2022) and ospina et al. (2022) maximize the number of travelers who can make their entire trip on
‘‘low stress links’’ or ‘‘safe infrastructure’’ under budget constraints. the papers thereby calculate the number of new trips covered
by safe infrastructure per investment.
another research avenue within the field of or aims at minimizing total generalized cost (sometimes simply traveled distance) in
the network subject to budget constraints. this is accomplished while taking the demand on specific routes of each origin–destination
pair into account. the research can be attributed to smith and haghani (2012) and mesbah et al. (2012), and has been developed
further in e.g. mauttone et al. (2017), liu et al. (2019) with multi-objective variations found in lin and yu (2013), lin and liao
(2016), liaw and lin (2022). none of the studies maximize societal cost–benefit measures.
a final contribution in the literature worthy of attention is liu et al. (2022). they set up a complex optimization problem with
the aim of finding a bicycle investment plan for chicago subject to budget constraints that maximizes bicycle ridership increase. this
is measured through a mode choice model embedded within the optimization framework, and the resulting bicycle plan increases
the bicycle ridership by more than 40%. the societal performance of the investment plan is not evaluated.
as our literature review shows, although several performance measures have been considered, none of the or studies calculate
the societal cost–benefit performance of the respective bicycle network investment plans. thus, economic efficiency of bicycle
investment plans has been largely absent in these existing studies. in this paper, we integrate the problem in a cost–benefit
framework, in which construction and maintenance costs of upgrades are compared to consumer surplus benefits and subjected
to budget constraints.
a final observation is that all of the or studies incorporate the choice of route directly into the optimization problem, sometimes
even using complex route choice models as the path-size logit model in liu et al. (2019). while this allows the objective function to
be evaluated exactly, it is not a feasible solution strategy for the number of links and origin–destination pairs presented in this paper
(refer to section 4). in fact, all studies except liu et al. (2022) consider only few origin–destination pairs or small networks. in this
paper, we instead propose a linear approximation of the consumer surplus that can be solved easily even for very large networks
and does not require embedding the route choice directly in the optimization model. evidence that the approximation error in doing
so is minuscule is provided in section 4.1.1.
3. methodology
the methodology section is divided into three parts. in section 3.1 we formulate a series of mathematical programs that describe
how the optimal selection of network segments can be determined. section 3.2 explains how we solve the problems and presents
basic reference strategies, whereas section 3.3 formulates a methodology for determining network segments (bundles of links) that
further reduce the dimension of the problem.
3.1. problem formulation
we use multiple intermediate steps to arrive at an optimal model suitable for large-scale application. the intermediate steps
are necessary to understand the idea behind the final solution approach. we propose the general problem in section 3.1.1, and
contextualize it in section 3.1.2. hereafter it is simplified assuming fixed demand and a linear approximation of consumer surplus
in section 3.1.3. section 3.1.4 formulates the problem as a markov chain, before we reformulate the problem as a selection of
network segments rather than individual links in section 3.1.5.
a presentation of the overall model-flow is presented in fig. 2. it illustrates the key-components included in the analysis and
the sequence in which they are calculated. importantly, the model optimizes the expected net present value (npv) at the end of
the evaluation period. the npv is a measure that takes into account all benefits and costs accumulated throughout the evaluation
period on a common scale and discount these to a single unified number, that summarizes the performance of a project in terms of
the monetized net present value of the investment (dasgupta and pearce, 1972; leleur, 2000), see further details in appendix b.1.
this suggests that a value-function, based on npv is a meaningful measure to maximize.
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778

m. paulsen and j. rich
fig. 2. a graphical synthesis of the final optimization approach of section 3.1.5. effects regarding consumer surplus (travel time savings), maintenance costs,
construction costs, and scrap value are calculated to determine the expected change in net present value in all future time periods. this measure is used as
the objective function in the budget constrained optimization that determines the optimal segments at time 𝑡. the resulting contribution is a consumer surplus
approximation, which is based solely on traffic assignments of two extreme scenarios: the original network and the fully upgraded network.
3.1.1. general problem formulation
we consider a network consisting of links𝑙 ∈ n, out of which the links𝑙 ∈ l can be upgraded. we then formulate the optimization
problem 3.1.1 as a decision process, where we determine the optimal activation of links 𝑙 ∈ l at each time period 𝑡 ∈ t. this is
measured through a binary decision variables 𝑢𝑡
𝑙 and an objective function that measures the societal discounted cost–benefit 𝑓𝑡
over all time periods (eq. (p1a)). we denote 𝐮𝑡 as the vector of decision variables across all 𝑙 ∈ l. the problem is constrained by
the costs of the upgrades 𝐶𝑡(𝐮𝑡), such that costs spent up to time 𝑡 cannot exceed the available budget 𝐵𝑡 (eq. (p1b)). the decision
variable decides whether to upgrade link 𝑙 ∈ l in time period 𝑡 ∈ t. initially, no links are upgraded (eq. (p1c)). once a link has
been upgraded, it stays like that for the rest of the period, leading to constraint eq. (p1d). finally, the decision variable is binary
(eq. (p1e)).

the complexity of the problem arises from how𝐶𝑡 and especially𝑓𝑡 are formulated. in general,𝑓𝑡 is determined through complex
models dependent on 𝐮𝑡. in the following section, we provide relevant approximations of these functions to facilitate large-scale

3.1.2. npv-model with flexible demand
in the following, we set up a mathematical program (problem 3.1.2) where we unfold the objective function as the expected
npv-contribution for time period𝑡 denoted by𝑍𝑡(𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1,𝛥 𝐮𝑡). here,𝛥𝐮𝑡 (p2i) is a binary vector indicating which links to upgrade in
time period𝑡. the net present value contribution explicitized in (eq. (p2b)) includes five terms: (i) the consumer surplus𝛥𝐶𝑆 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1),
for which we apply the rule-of-the-half approximation (kidokoro, 2004) based on the travel costs (eq. (p2c)), see also appendix b.2;
(ii) the health benefits 𝛥𝐻𝐵 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1) which are proportional to the increase in traveled kilometers by bicycle (eq. (p2d)), (iii) the
construction costs 𝐶𝐶 (𝛥𝐮𝑡) (eq. (p2e)), (iv) the maintenance costs 𝑀𝐶 (𝐮𝑡−1) (eq. (p2f)) and, finally, (v) the scrap values 𝑆𝑉 (𝛥𝐮𝑡)
(eq. (p2g)), which is the direct value of investments discounted to the end of the evaluation period (boardman et al., 2018).

transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778
7
m. paulsen and j. rich
consumer surplus, health benefits, and maintenance costs apply to all links that have been upgraded up until time 𝑡 − 1, i.e. they
are functions of 𝐮𝑡−1, whereas the construction costs and scrap value only apply to the links upgraded in time period𝑡 (are functions
of 𝛥𝐮𝑡).
we do not take into account any possible negative effects on other means of transportation, particularly cars and public
transport, when calculating the npv-contribution. the primary reason for this is that we are mainly considering upgrades of existing
infrastructure in the denser urban areas. hence, capacity on the primary road network is unlikely to be affected in any significant
way. for upgrades in the outskirts of the city, space is less of an issue, as is congestion. most of the planned upgrades in these areas
are implemented parallel to the existing network and will therefore not hinder car traffic. while the inclusion of car traffic can be
neglected in the current context, there can be situations where this is not the case. in section 5.1 where future research topics are
covered, we elaborate in more details on this. furthermore, in appendix b.1.1 we discuss which factors to include when calculating
the npv.
for 𝑡 = 1 , the npv-contribution 𝑍1(𝑡, 𝐮0,𝛥 𝐮1) depends on the initial network 𝐮0 and the changes made to the network during
the first period 𝛥𝐮1. in future time periods, the npv-contribution does not only depend on the upgrades in time period time 𝑡 (𝛥𝐮𝑡),
but also on the network condition, in terms of which links have been upgraded in the previous time period ( 𝐮𝑡−1). the sum of
npv-contributions across all time periods 𝑍 = ∑
𝑡∈t𝑍𝑡(𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1,𝛥 𝐮𝑡) then represents the predicted npv for the system, and is the
objective function maximized in the mathematical program (eq. (p2b)).
the problem is restricted by a budget constraint (eq. (p2h)), that ensures that, for each time period, the total expenditure of
new upgrades and the expenditure used for maintenance of existing upgrades, cannot exceed the corresponding budget 𝐵𝑡.
the starting value of 𝐮 is zero for all links 𝑙 ∈ l (eq. (p2k)). for links that cannot be upgraded, i.e. 𝑙 ∈ n ⧵ l, we furthermore
have that 𝑢𝑡
𝑙 remains zero throughout the entire evaluation period (eq. (p2j)).
the function of demand 𝑥𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡) and travel time savings ∑
𝑙∈n𝑃 0

𝑙∈n𝑃𝑙,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝐮𝑡−1)
(
𝑝𝑙,𝑘 +𝑢𝑡−1

are functions
of the decision variable 𝐮𝑡 in a highly non-linear fashion. these non-linear dependencies are expressed through the link probability
function𝑃𝑙,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝐮𝑡), which for any given solution 𝐮𝑡 expresses the probability of using link𝑙 for traveler of type𝑘 who travels from𝑖 to
𝑗. this introduce substantial complexity to the problem as it requires embedding the route choice model as part of the mathematical
program. this lead to|n|×|k|×|𝛺| additional decision variables per time period: one for each link for each traveler type and for each
od pair. in our case study, this would correspond to more than 100 billion additional decision variables and not be computationally
feasible.
we note that although formulated through a zone-based setup (with traveler types𝑘 traveling between𝑖 and𝑗), the methodology
would be identical for an agent-based setup. here, k would be the population of agents, and there would exist a 𝛺𝑘 for each agent
containing the origins and destinations of that particular agent. for od-matrices with many small entries below one it may in fact
reduce the computational overhead.
3.1.3. npv-model with fixed demand and approximated consumer surplus
in order to make the problem more manageable, we propose the following changes which are incorporated in problem :
• we assume that the demand is fixed, i.e. 𝑥𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡) = 𝑥0
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘, ∀𝑡 ∈ t. the demand still varies across combinations of origins,
destinations, and traveler types, but does not change when the network is upgraded. this causes an underestimation of the
consumer surpluses and causes the total bicycle mileage to remain largely constant across the different time periods.
• as a direct consequence of the above, the health benefits𝛥𝐻𝐵𝑡 can be removed from the objective function, as induced bicycle
traffic demand is the only meaningful way health benefits are affected.
• to avoid complex non-linear functions in the consumer surplus function 𝛥𝐶𝑆𝑡 we apply a linear approximation denoted by
𝛥̃𝐶𝑆 . in this function, we do not consider link probabilities directly. instead, we use an approximation based on the shortest
path as described in greater detail below. this validity of the approximation will be scrutinized later in section 4.1.1.
the function 𝛥̃𝐶𝑆 (eq. (p3d)) approximates the consumer surplus by assuming that the od and traveler type-specific benefits
can be distributed along the corresponding shortest paths in the full scenario, where all links have been upgraded. we denote such a
shortest path by𝑞l
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘. the travel cost savings of traveler type𝑘, between origin𝑖 and𝑗, in the full scenario – compared to the network
without any upgrades – are defined as the term
(∑
𝑙′∈l𝑃 0
𝑙′,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘𝑝𝑙′,𝑘 −∑
𝑙′∈𝑞l
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘
̂ 𝑝𝑙′,𝑘

factor 𝐿𝑙∑

𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 ∩l𝐿𝑙′
to ensure that benefits are distributed proportionally to the length of the links and to prevent double counting
the benefits. finally, by summing across all traveler types and od pairs, we arrive at the approximate consumer surplus ̃ 𝑠𝑙, that is
deemed to arise from upgrading link 𝑙 ∈ l.
it follows directly that the approximated consumer surplus in time period 𝑡 is equal to 𝛥̃𝐶𝑆 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1) = 𝜅𝑡

upgraded. clearly, for solutions within these two extremes it remains an open question how good the approximation is. however, in
section 4.1.1 we evaluate the approximation by comparing it to the correct consumer surplus function found through proper traffic
assignment and shows that the approximation is surprisingly good.
the great advantage of the approximated consumer surplus of link 𝑙 (̃ 𝑠𝑙) is that it is entirely independent of network upgrades
𝐮𝑡 for all 𝑡 ∈ t, which means that it can be computed in advance. all it takes is the following two stages. first, assign traffic onto

the base network and the full network and store the resulting travel costs differences (benefits) for all traveler types 𝑘 ∈ k and all
od pairs𝑖,𝑗 ∈𝛺. this is normally carried out in a standard cost–benefit analyses when performing a full one-shot evaluation of the
entire project portfolio l compared to a baseline (leleur, 2000; kidokoro, 2004; boardman et al., 2018). secondly, we assign the
corresponding benefits back onto the network while correcting for network lengths and keeping track of the accumulated benefits
on each link.

as problem is a linear binary integer program it is much simpler to solve compared to problem 3.1.2. however, still the problem
is dynamic and with a large solution space when solved over many time periods simultaneously. in the next section, we propose an
alternative formulation where the problem is decomposed into a sequence of linear binary integer programs, where the solution for
𝑡 − 1 feeds into the problem for 𝑡. hence, the problem is re-stated as a markov-chain type of problem, which reduces the calculation
complexity significantly.

3.1.4. a markov chain formulation
in the following, we frame the problem as a sequential problem where the selected upgrades in time period 𝑡 condition the
investment strategy for stage 𝑡 + 1 and so on. this is a common approach when approximating complex dynamic problems as
described in powell (2011) among others. the approach is sometimes referred to as the policy iteration algorithm as described
in sutton and barto (2018). the step-wise optimization sequence is given by;

what comes directly after, namely stage 𝑡 + 1. hence, the process is essentially a dynamic markov chain over a number of stages.
however, in order to solve the problem in a meaningful way it is required that we consider future consequences when selecting
network upgrades at time𝑡. as upgrades have to be built before they have an impact, maintenance costs and consumer surplus only
depend on what was upgraded in all previous time periods. by definition then, they are not affected by any choices made at time
𝑡, which obviously would lead to a markov chain with little meaning.
as a consequence, we change the formulation of ̃𝑍𝑡 (eq. (p3b)) and create ̃𝑍𝐹

and maintenance cost of existing upgraded links at time 𝑡, but also future benefits and costs for all future 𝑡. this is easily done by

3.1.5. formulation with network segmentation
since the problem has no awareness of the network, as the individual link probabilities are not explicitly calculated, and the
problem does not consider links that are not part of the portfolio l, the problem can be transformed to consider bundles of links
𝑏 ∈ b rather than individual links 𝑙 ∈ l. we refer to such bundles as network segments.
the problem is reformulated by aggregating most link-specific variables and decision variables to the level of segments by
summing across the links present in the segment, e.g. 𝑐𝑏 = ∑
𝑙∈𝑏𝑐𝑙 for construction costs. the same goes with the decision
variables, where we now consider choices of segments 𝑢𝑡
𝑏 rather than links, causing the size of 𝐮𝑡 to be lowered considerably
from the number of potential link upgrades |l| to the number of network segments |b|. notice that the path-based cost savings

10
m. paulsen and j. rich
(∑
𝑙∈l𝑃 0
𝑙,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘𝑝𝑙,𝑘 −∑
𝑙∈𝑞l
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘
̂ 𝑝𝑙,𝑘
)
continue to be evaluated based on the individual links of the network prior to the optimization. the
resulting measure ̃ 𝑠𝑏 (eq. (p5c)) is aggregated across all links in each network segment 𝑙 ∈𝑏, though.
the final sequential problem based on network segments is stated in problem 3.1.5. in section 3.3, we propose a method for
segmenting links that dramatically reduces the size of the problem from approximately 30,000 to 202 decision variables.

3.2. problem solving
as argued previously, the mathematical programs in problem and problem 3.1.5 can be solved sequentially as a series of linear
binary integer programming problems. there exist several well-tested solution algorithms for this type of problems. in this paper
we solve the problem using the ‘‘lpsolve’’ package (berkelaar et al., 2022) in r (r core team, 2023), which utilizes the branch and
bound method for integer programming (land and doig, 1960). we will refer to this solution by blp (binary linear programming).
furthermore, we also propose two alternative approximate solution approaches in which the segments are selected according
to a greedy algorithm. in this case, we always select the segment that increases the objective function ̃𝑍𝑡
𝑓 the most per unit of
construction cost. we denote such a segment by 𝑏𝛥𝐮𝑡
∗ and define it using mathematical notation as,

small, and using the construction costs alone eventually resulted in slightly higher final net present values.

3.2.1. five basic reference strategies
we compare our optimized solutions to five basic reference strategies that do not take the expected benefits of segments into
account. there are two reasons for doing this. firstly, we do it in order to evaluate how the expected consumer surplus based on
a linear approximation deviate from a properly evaluated consumer surplus in many different situations, and not only in or close
to the optimal frontier (see section 4.1.1). secondly, we do it to investigate the performance gap between optimal and pure ad-hoc
approaches. we will later see in section 4.3.1 , that the performance of the actual development of the cycle superhighway network
in copenhagen (actual order, see appendix c for a description) lies within (the upper part of) the range of the five basic reference
strategies described in this section.
as cycle superhighway networks are often developed one route at the time, we include two of such strategies: one that
implements the routes according to their route lengths in ascending order (shorter routes first), and one that does so in descending
order ( longer routes first ). another approach is to ignore route structures, and instead base the development of the cycle
superhighway network on the lengths of the network segments themselves. again we do so in both ascending ( shorter segments
first) and descending (longer segments first) order. finally, our fifth reference strategy simply selects the next segment randomly
among the remaining segments ( random order).
fig. 3. existing and planned future cycle superhighway network segments for the greater copenhagen area ( sekretariatet for supercykelstier , 2019 ). the
combination of all 202 network segments constitute a network of approximately 580 km of proposed bicycle infrastructure of which only the first three segments
are illustrated in the legend. (for interpretation of the references to color in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the web version of this article.)
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778

m. paulsen and j. rich
3.3. network segmentation
in the process of developing heuristics for the temporal and geographical performance of network segments, it has become clear
that a clustering of links into network segments is relevant (liu et al., 2019). especially since the network we use for our case study
in section 4 has a much higher resolution (many more links) than normally seen in the literature for similar problems. the proposed
total bicycle superhighway network expansion consists of 21,658 potential links of which many are very short and essentially belong
to the same path.
in this paper we take a simple approach regarding the clustering of network links. we begin at the origin of the first proposed
cycle superhighway route and continue along the path until we meet or intersect the existing cycle superhighway network (or any
previously processed proposed cycle superhighway route). whenever such intersection is met, we give the traversed part of the path
a unique segment id and initiate a new segment where the previous segment ended. once we reach the destination of the proposed
cycle superhighway route, we continue the procedure for the remaining proposed routes. clearly, for cycle superhighway routes that
were already processed, there is a risk that the cycle superhighway route under investigation will intersect them. when this happen,
not only the current segment, but also the existing segment of the already processed cycle superhighway route is split in two. the
network segmentation is carried out in arcgis, by manually identifying and spatially selecting each of the network segments and
assigning them a unique segment id.
as the choice of segmentation and the network aggregation are likely to affect the resulting solutions, the segmentation should
comply with the type of investments considered. we believe that this is the case for our approach, as cycle superhighway routes are
intended to connect two specific urban areas. therefore, if a route does not intersect with existing or planned cycle superhighway
infrastructure, splitting it in two would not make sense. on the other hand, if the route crosses existing or other planned cycle
superhighway routes, splitting it in two could make sense, as it could connect different urban areas.
however, to ensure compliance with the budget constraints in our case study, we divided all segments with a construction cost
above 40 million dkk into smaller sections prior to the optimization. this applied to four segments, which were split at appropriate
villages or crossroads.
the total set of possible segments (denoted by b) is shown in fig. 3 as an overlay on the existing cycle superhighway network.
in total, 202 segments were identified with an accumulated length of 580 km. it is noteworthy how the segments get shorter as
they get closer to the urban centre of copenhagen. this is because the density of the proposed routes is higher in this part of the
network, leading to more intersecting routes and a more decomposed route structure.
notice, that the segmentation of links is only a means to reduce the size of the solution space with respect to proposed extensions.
cyclists that travel in the network still select their route in a fully disaggregate network (consisting of more than 400,000 links in
our case study in section 4.
4. case study: copenhagen super cycle highway network
in the following we consider a case study for the greater copenhagen region, which is concerned with the development of a large
cycle superhighway network (sekretariatet for supercykelstier, 2019). the network, which is partitioned into segments in fig. 3,
consists of 55 proposed extensions and therefore represents numerous intermediate planning stages. the challenge is to design the
investment portfolio over time such that the net present value of investments is optimized. section 4.1 analyzes network-assigned
travel time savings, and justifies using these as a basis for our approximative consumer surplus. section 4.2 states the most important
limitations of the case study, whereas section 4.3 presents and analyzes the results of the optimization-based solutions.
4.1. network-assigned benefits
fig. 4 illustrates how benefits for specific links are distributed in terms of the total amount of benefit flowing across each link
of the network. this is similar to the measure ̃ 𝑠𝑏 in eq. (p5c), but without the term
∑
𝑙∈𝑏𝐿𝑙∑
𝑙′ ∈𝑞l
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 ∩l𝐿𝑙′
that prevents double counting (see
section 3.1.3). due to this, each link along the shortest path receives the entire benefit. also, in the figure, benefits are assigned
to all links in the shortest paths network and not only on network segments corresponding to proposed network upgrades. the
wide blue links represent the added infrastructure, while thin green links represent links that appear in the baseline. darker colors
represent higher benefits (measured in thousand dkk per year) as opposed to light colors. as can be seen, the network is very
detailed, consisting of a total of 471,356 links.
several things can be revealed from this figure. firstly, it is clear that there are a number of very poor performing links, especially
those links in the north-western outskirts of the region. it is also worth noting that benefits are present both in the ‘old’ and the ‘new’
infrastructure. this is because the surplus is measured at the level of od. hence, all links on a given route for an od combination
will benefit even if only a subset of these links are changed.
fig. 4 also indirectly indicates, which strategies might be useful when considering different investment stages for the infrastruc-
ture. connectedness and correlation between links is important in this respect. if a group of links are disconnected from other links,
further development within this infrastructure is essentially a separate sub-problem, which is disconnected from the larger network.
on the other hand, for links that are dependent, the analysis is more complicated as adding these links may influence the benefit
of other links, underlining the complexity of the problem at hand.

4.1.1. validation of network-assigned benefits as a linear approximation of consumer surplus
the cost-related components (maintenance costs, construction costs, and scrap value) of problem 3.1.5 are identical to those
of problem 3.1.2, and the effect of these can therefore be evaluated ‘‘exactly’’ conditional on the remaining inputs. instead, the
approximation error of our simplified problem comes from the linear approximation of the consumer surplus, and by assuming
constant demand. therefore, putting the constant demand aside (see section 4.2 for a further discussion on this topic), we can
claim that if the approximated consumer surplus (in problem 3.1.5 ) is close to the properly evaluated consumer surplus, then any
good solution to problem 3.1.5 is also a good solution to the general problem 3.1.2 .
as a result, in this section, we evaluate how well our linear approximation corresponds to the correct expected consumer surplus
function. to do this, for all the strategies and solutions from our long-term investment scenario presented in section 4.3.2 , we
evaluate the consumer surplus in each year according to the methodology from hallberg et al. ( 2021) (see also appendix b.2 ),
and compare these to the corresponding values of our expected consumer surplus based on linear approximations 𝛥̃𝐶𝑆 (𝑡, 𝐮𝑡−1) =
𝜅𝑡
∑
𝑏∈b𝑢𝑡−1
𝑏 ̃ 𝑠𝑏.
first, we compare the two consumer surplus approaches visually by plotting the two measures for each 𝑡 for all strategies in
fig. 5. although the similarities are higher for some strategies than others (e.g. better for longer segments first than shorter
routes first), overall, there is a striking similarity between the two approaches.
to support the visual findings and to quantify the differences between the two consumer surplus functions, in table 1 we calculate
various relevant metrics. an intuitive metric is the 𝑧-statistics, which can facilitate a paired 𝑧-test between the two approaches.

however, as the 𝑧-test only considers absolute (not relative) deviations between the two measures, the test almost always finds
that the values of the two approaches differ, with |𝑧|-statistics generally ranging between about 2.9 and 4.6. an exception to this is
longer segments first, for which the difference is insignificant with a |𝑧|-statistic of 0.11 corresponding to a 𝑝-value of 0.91.
as an alternative comparison, we instead consider three metrics that also take the overall level of the two series into account:
normalized mean absolute error (mae), normalized root-mean-square error (nrmse), and the relative error (re). the measures
have been calculated for each strategy/solution using each of the non-trivial entries of consumer surplus. by this, we mean in all
years 𝑡 in which some upgrades were performed in year 𝑡 − 1 (𝑡 ∈ t ∶ ∑
𝑏∈b𝛥𝑢𝑡−1
𝑏 > 0). the numbers support that the relative
difference between the two measures is indeed small. this strongly suggests that our linear approximation of consumer surplus is
justified, and thus, that the solutions obtained by solving problem 3.1.5 would be good solutions to the correct but slightly more
complex problem.
4.2. limitations
after having evaluated the performance of our approximate optimization heuristics, it is relevant to mention that the present
paper does not consider induced traffic or external costs from network changes. including these effects, would make it necessary
to model demand responses in a separate model. this would complicate the modeling setup and would also make it difficult to
disentangle the different parts in the effect analysis. following this, our analysis focuses solely on the direct network related benefits
that are related to travel time savings as in hallberg et al. (2021), see appendix b.2. as we consider upgrade of existing bicycle
infrastructure or construction of new cycleways in rural areas, we also ignore any changes in travel times for other modes as these
are considered negligible.
as demonstrated in rich et al. (2021), it implies that our benefit estimates are substantially underestimated. however, it allows
a specific focus on the network related benefits. external benefits that arise from improved public health are directly proportional
to the increase in bicycle mileage. the integration of such benefits requires an additional modeling effort to capture mode-choice
and destination substitution effects, and to take these into consideration in the optimization. see section 5 for a discussion of future
work.
4.3. assessment of optimal investment strategies
in the following we evaluate the societal cost–benefit performance of the solutions to the optimization problems proposed in
section 3.1 in various experiments. we also compare them to the five alternative baseline investment strategies from section 3.2.1
and the actual observed development of the cycle superhighway network documented in appendix c.
we test the strategies in a case study with the 202 potential network segments introduced in section 3.3, and an evaluation
period of 50 years ( |t| = 50 ) divided into 50 equally sized investment stages, each with dedicated cumulative budgets. in each
investment stage, network segments can be built as long as the costs of the segments do not exceed the budget (constraint (p5g) of
problem 3.1.5). as the budgets are cumulative, excess unused budgets from previous investment stages are transferred to subsequent
stages. for simplicity, we assume that the cumulative budgets increase linearly in time, i.e.𝐵𝑡 =𝑘𝑡 for some𝑘. in appendix c, based
on the actual development of the cycle superhighway network in recent years, we find an appropriate value to be𝑘 = 50 mill. dkk.
the construction costs and maintenance costs of the 202 segments are based on the costs of incentive (2018). as incentive
(2018) calculated costs at the level of routes, the cost of each segment is approximated by adopting the price per km per year for
the corresponding bicycle superhighway route. the actual cost of each segment is then calculated by multiplying with the length
of the segment. maps visualizing the costs per km can be found in appendix d.
the reported consumer surpluses are found following the approach of hallberg et al. (2021) for each year of the evaluation
period. that is, the route choices are properly updated after each year of expansion considering the entire network, and travel time
savings are calculated accordingly. see also appendix b.2. the shortest path searches needed to calculate the travel times and travel

binary linear programs are solved using the package ‘‘lpsolve’’ (berkelaar et al., 2022). 2
a short description of the nine applied strategies can be found in table 2 alongside an indication of performance with regards
to final net present values (npv) and benefit–cost ratios (bcr). formal descriptions of npv and bcr can be found in appendix b.1.
we consider three different variations of the case study, each corresponding to one column of results in table 2. first, in
section 4.3.1 we consider a scenario, where investments can only be carried out during the first five years. in section 4.3.2 we
relax this constraint and evaluate long-term investment plans. finally, in section 4.3.3 we explore how well our solution strategies
recover from a bad starting strategy.
4.3.1. five year investment period (comparison to actual recent development)
our case study is based on data from hallberg et al. (2021), which used data from 2019. since then the copenhagen cycle
superhighway has been extended by a few routes, see appendix c for further details.
therefore, we set up an initial scenario, where we compare the optimal solution from our model with the corresponding actual
observed development of the cycle superhighway network in the same period. for the comparison to be fair, we restrict upgrades
to only be conducted during the first five years of the evaluation period, i.e. 𝑢𝑡
𝑙 =𝑢5
𝑙, ∀𝑙 ∈ l, ∀𝑡 ∈ t ∶𝑡 >5. this also requires that
the corresponding budget needs to be exactly high enough to cover the costs of the actual upgrades in this period. otherwise, the
performance of the actual development would be uncomparable to the other strategies. as seen in appendix c, this implies budgets
given by 𝐵𝑡 = 46.2𝑡. although investments can only be implemented in the first five years, we still evaluate the projects over a
50 years period.
table 3 presents selected key performance indicators, as well as the magnitude of the underlying benefits and costs needed to
calculate them, for the different investment strategies. it is seen that the consumer surplus of the optimization solutions are much
larger compared to the actual order and the five basic reference strategies. although the optimization strategies invest in slightly
more expensive segments, they still lead to considerably higher net present values of almost 1.2 bill. dkk. this is significantly higher
than the best reference strategy ( shorter segments first) for which the net present value is only 302 mill. dkk.
the actual development is beneficial from a societal point-of-view, achieving a positive net present value of 102 mill. dkk
despite only including travel time benefits. however, although its net present value is almost 500 mill. dkk higher than the poorest
performing reference strategy ( longer segments first), it performs 200 mill. dkk worse than the best performing basic reference
strategy (shorter segments first), and is more than an order of magnitude worse than the optimized solutions in terms of net present
value. since the actual development – and the best performing basic reference strategy – are actually performing quite decently, it
indicates that the optimized solutions indeed seem to provide reasonable solutions.

4.3.2. analysis of long-term implementation schemes
we now consider a long-term investment strategy, where investments can be made in any year in the evaluation period. we
consider budgets of 𝐵𝑡 = 50𝑡 (see appendix c), leading to a maximum budget of 2.5 bill. dkk. this is sufficient to invest the entire
project portfolio. as the actual development is unknown beyond year 5, actual order is not included in the case study variation
(nor in the final variation in section 4.3.3).
this allows us to investigate two additional effects. firstly, we are able to isolate and investigate the impact of the order of
investments, as for six of the eight strategies (all except blp and greedy w/ stop), the entire portfolio is built. as such, the only
thing that differs across these strategies, is the order in which the investments are made. secondly, we can evaluate the added effect
of also discontinuing to invest once it is no longer found profitable under the assumptions of the problems.
key performance indicators of the scenarios are found in table 4. the net present values and benefit–cost ratios clearly vary
substantially over the eight strategies, even when only considering the six strategies that upgrade the entire portfolio. this supports
our claim that the order of implementation does indeed have an enormous impact on the resulting societal cost–benefit performance
with differences of more than 1.8 bill. dkk in terms of net present value. this underlines the importance of prioritizing the order
of investment appropriately.
the relative effect of maintaining a model-based investment strategy is surprisingly large. compared to the best basic reference
strategy (shorter segments first ), the net present value increases by 750 mill. dkk, even when investing in the entire portfolio.
unsurprisingly, the effect of stopping investments when segments are no longer beneficial is also large. this causes the net present
values to increase by additional 875 mill. dkk, with a surplus of more than 2.7 bill. dkk compared to the poorest strategy ( longer
segments first). solving the optimization problem through a greedy strategy give largely similar results compared to the results of
the optimal solution to the mathematical program.
if looking in more details at the different aspects of costs and benefits, it is revealed that the construction costs differ across the
investment strategies. this is caused by the different cost-profiles for the different segments (as seen in fig. d.1) which influences
the discounted value of the construction costs. as seen, construction costs are highest for the strategies that invest in the entire
portfolio. greedy w/o stop , shorter segments first , and shouter routes first have the highest construction costs, as they tend
to place investments in densely populated areas closer to the city center in the early stages of the investment plan. in terms of
maintenance costs, greedy w/o stop has the lowest maintenance costs among the six strategies that invest in the entire portfolio.
this indicates, that the maintenance cost, which is paid annually for the remainder of the evaluation period, plays a crucial part
when determining the optimal order.
the consumer surpluses are all positive and reflect that whenever infrastructure is improved, users are always better off. however,
the results clearly suggest that benefits are outweighed by costs for all strategies, except for the optimized strategies, and that the
consumer surplus varies a lot across the different strategies. the difference between greedy w/ stop and greedy w/o stop shows
that although the remaining segments are unprofitable, they still accumulate substantial consumer surplus.

m. paulsen and j. rich
fig. 6. net present value in every year of the evaluation period for each of the eight strategies.
fig. 7. benefit–cost ratio in every year of the evaluation period for each of the eight strategies.
fig. 6 illustrates the net present value for every year over the evaluation period for all of the eight investment strategies. likewise,
fig. 7 presents the accumulated benefit–cost ratio throughout the evaluation period. as can be seen from the figures, the order of
the performance of the different strategies largely remains unchanged in the entire period. it means that irrespective of the historical
investments up to a given year, if the investment strategy that follows in proceeding years is properly optimized, it will outperform

the five basic reference strategies. it is also seen that greedy w/o stop performs very well until year 8, after which the other two
optimization solutions benefit from eliminating investments in unprofitable segments and, from this point on, ‘harvest’ accumulated
benefits from the first investments.

however, it should be noticed here that since benefits only include the direct network-related benefits (see section 4.2), and are
thereby largely underestimated, many of the projects after year 8 are likely to be beneficial as well. hence, the main take-away of
the presented results is that we can expect massive differences in the accumulated benefits across different investment strategies.
the effect of stopping investments that are unprofitable is even more prevalent, when considering cost–benefits ratios (bcr)
as in fig. 7. apart from that, the same tendencies as described for the npv holds, even though the solution strategies were not
optimized with respect to the bcr. this is also evident from the figure around year 8, where an acceleration of bcr is seen for blp
and greedy w/o stop. this indicates that the last many upgrades for these strategies were decreasing the overall bcr.
in fact, the optimal solution based on maximizing bcr would most likely be to only invest in a single segment; namely the one
that has the highest accumulated consumer surplus per accumulated cost. however, clearly, from a societal point-of-view such a
solution is of limited interest and would yield a very small net present value.
we illustrate different investment stages geographically for two selected investment strategies (bpl and longer segments first)
in fig. 8. needless to say, from the perspective of the society, this information is vital as it suggests where to start the investments
and, equally important, where we should postpone investments. the suggested network extension reveals several things. firstly, it
suggests that connectedness to the existing cycle superhighway infrastructure is important. many of the existing cycle superhighways
connect the city center and the suburbs, whereas there are fewer axial routes that go around. the analysis pinpoints that connecting
the direct existing cycle superhighways with axial routes is beneficial. another interesting observation in that regard is that the
optimal investment scheme in year 8, by and large seems to work as an extension of the investments made in year 4 and year 2.
secondly, it is seen that the optimal solution is to upgrade only to year 8. before year 8, investments are primarily carried out
in denser urban areas closer to the city center, with only few upgrades in rural and less densely populated suburbs (as opposed
to longer segments first ). this is not surprising and raises an important discussion concerning the regional and geographical
distribution of investments. clearly, from a political standpoint it could be difficult to defend investment only in densely populated
areas, and this then raises a discussion of what should be the trade-off between pure societal cost–benefit performance and regional
concerns. we return to this discussion in section 5.
4.3.3. independence of base network
the case study has also been evaluated in a scenario where the strategies are deliberately initiated with a poor starting strategy.
to accommodate this, we use the solution of the random order strategy in a period of 10 years (see fig. e.1 in appendix e). we
denote these initial solutions by 𝑢𝑡,𝐑𝐚𝐧𝐝𝐨𝐦
𝑏 , and impose 𝑢𝑡
𝑏 =𝑢𝑡,𝐑𝐚𝐧𝐝𝐨𝐦
𝑏 , ∀𝑏 ∈ b for 𝑡 ≤ 10. this allows us to test how well the solution
strategies are able to recover from a poor starting strategy. the corresponding key figures are shown in table 5.
forcing a poor starting strategy naturally leads to a worse performance, as such strategy tends to implement many unprofitable
segments in the start, which accumulate only few benefits going forward. interestingly, despite upgrading more segments, due to
the timing and discounting of the upgrades the corresponding consumer surpluses actually decreases by more than 480 mill. dkk
compared to the unrestricted case study variation (table 4). the profitable segments are simply upgraded too late to yield a very high
consumer surplus. obviously, this, combined with the aforementioned cost increases, affect the net present values and benefit–cost
ratios negatively. both measures decrease considerably, with net present values dropping by more than 1 bill. dkk.
however, most importantly, table 5 clearly illustrates that the method replicate the tendencies of the previous tests. this
demonstrates that the superiority of the optimization heuristics is invariant to whatever solution we currently have. it basically
means that irrespective of which segments have currently been upgraded an optimization based strategy is expected to render
massive performance gains going forward from that point on. this obviously gives credibility to the idea of framing the problem as
a markov chain (eq. (1)).
5. discussion
in practice, when investing in infrastructure over a long time horizon, it involves, as a first stage, an initial plan of where to
start. however, as it is difficult to predict transport demand for future years (in particular if these are far into the future), a good
planning practice will tend to re-estimate and recalculate strategies as information underlying the initial decision is updated. it
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778
21
m. paulsen and j. rich
might very well be that due to external conditions, such as changed location patterns of the population or parallel investments in
other transport networks, investment schemes for the bicycle infrastructure need to be reassessed. the framework that is presented
in this paper allows exactly this, in that it is clearly shown that irrespective of where we start from, our optimization-based solutions
are superior to the reference strategies when going forward. it means that the framework can be used to extend networks in any
year and for any prior investment strategy.
in the paper, we show that the order of investments have a strong impact on the net present value. this underlines the importance
and relevance of proper prioritization and also shows that one-shot investment plans as presented in rich et al. (2021) will be
inefficient. furthermore, our optimization strategy allows us to determine when to stop investments, namely when investments no
longer contribute positively to the net present value.
the strength of the proposed method is when it is applied to large and long-term network expansions. hence, if networks for
new cities or large urban areas are planned in investment stages, it makes sense to apply our approach to optimize the development
plan. for smaller more ‘surgical’ network upgrades, there are fewer degrees of freedom in the solution space, why the effect will be
lower.
as opposed to rich et al. (2021), in the paper we deliberately focus on direct travel time savings resulting from changes to the
network. hence, we restrict the analysis from other demand effects that could arise because of mode- and destination substitution
(see section 4.2). this is obviously a limitation, in particularly because health benefits resulting from increased bicycle use often
dominate the societal benefits of bicycle infrastructure investments (rich et al., 2021). hence, this study represents a natural lower
bound for the societal benefits and net present values that can likely be achieved if all combined demand effects were integrated in
the surplus calculation.
another consequence is that the investment plans may partly suffer from bias towards upgrading the network at locations where
the number of cyclists is already high. this is where the consumer surplus benefits will be largest under the assumption of constant
demand. in reality, however, this might not always be the best strategy, as also suggested by e.g. duthie and unnikrishnan (2014).
as health benefits are directly proportional to bicycle mileage, investing in areas where trips are longer but volumes are lower, can
be beneficial.
5.1. future research
a few obvious extensions of the presented approach can be mentioned. firstly, the integration of demand effects in order to
properly include health-related effects in the assessment. this will require that a demand model (i.e., choice of mode and destination)
is integrated as part of the model framework. it also requires the optimization framework to be able to forecast demand effects and
take such effects into account dynamically.
another avenue for future research is to apply more sophisticated route choice algorithms to include a richer substitution pattern
at the level of routes. there is nothing in the current model structure that prevents this although the choice-set generation can
become complicated because choice sets may need to be changed dynamically throughout the evaluation period. in this paper, for
simplicity and to keep focus on the optimization methods, in line with hallberg et al. (2021) and rich et al. (2021) we assume
that travel time is the only attribute influencing the route choice. however, taking into account additional link attributes would
increase the behavioral realism of the cyclists’ choice of route. the literature on this is rich (e.g. menghini et al., 2010; broach
et al., 2012; prato et al., 2018; fosgerau et al., 2023), and shows that other factors such as type of roads, amenities, and safety, also
plays an important role. as such, allowing more network attributes to influence the route choice of cyclists within the optimization
framework will also be relevant in future applications.
while in the current approach, we apply a zone-to-zone formulation of the problem, it is straightforward to re-formulate the
entire problem by looping over agents instead. in situations where there are many zones and other dimensions such as time-of-day
intervals, this may even bring down the computational complexity of calculating the approximated consumer surplus ̃ 𝑠𝑏.
it would also be relevant to investigate the effect of updating the linear approximations of consumer surplus dynamically,
e.g. after each time period. that is, assigning travel time benefits to the network based on the full scenario with all segments
upgraded (as currently) and the current solution up until 𝑡 (instead of the current baseline with no segments upgraded). although
it would likely improve the performance of the solutions, the similarity between our current linear approximations and the actual
consumer surplus function, as demonstrated in section 4.1.1, suggests that the potential performance gains might be limited.
another line of research left for the future, is the activation of spatial minimum budget constraints in addition to the already
existing maximum budget constraints. this could prevent neglecting less beneficial parts of a region; something that was found to
be the case for the optimal solution of this study. while the presented framework allows such constraints to be formulated, it will
be interesting to consider how such affect the optimum value and to what extent it changes the nature of the investments. as an
example, it will be possible to derive shadow prices for the spatial distribution of investments and these can then be compared to
the value of having geographically dispersed networks.
cities where the space for new bicycle infrastructure is not already allocated as separate bicycle lanes could warrant a
consideration of additional modes, particularly cars. this would add considerable complexity to the model as the travel time and
costs for car traffic depends on the car flow on any given link. thus, such travel time changes cannot, unlike those we measure for
bicycle traffic, be attributed solely to route choice changes, since the travel times of an od pair may change even when the route
choice of the od pair remains unchanged. for this reason, the methodology for approximating the consumer surplus for cyclists,
cannot in a straightforward manner be applied to car traffic. thus, it remains an open question how to include disbenefits for car
traffic in the optimization in case studies where this factor is deemed important.
transportation research part b 174 (2023) 102778
22
m. paulsen and j. rich
although the presented work focuses on the expansion of bicycle infrastructure, it has wider applications if applied to other
network expansions. it can, for instance, easily be adjusted for expanding public transport networks. when incorporating multiple
modes in the optimization problem, it is also a possibility to consider the optimization as a multi-objective problem, in the sense
that the different infrastructures will compete for space and capacity. by exploring the pareto-frontier between investments in the
different types of infrastructures, it is possible to approximate the optimal balance between these investments and the space they
take up.
6. conclusion
in the paper, we present a mathematical framework for optimizing the societal performance of long-term bicycle network
expansions in terms net present value. methodologically, the problem is framed as a dynamic optimization problem based on a
linear approximation of travel time savings, which is solved as a sequence of linear binary integer problems. it is demonstrated that
the linear approximation leads to very similar values when compared to a correctly evaluated measure of travel time savings where
route choices and travel times are recalculated every year.
the method is tested and evaluated by considering the development of a large-scale network of cycle superhighways in
copenhagen over a period of 50 years. by assuming annual investment budgets, we model which extensions to build each year.
hence, we answer the important question of ‘where’ and ‘when’ to invest and derive the accumulated societal benefits that result
from the different investment strategies.
in terms of societal cost–benefit performance, in a short-term comparison to the actual development of the cycle superhighway
network between 2019–2024, it is found that the optimized solutions render net present values close to 1.2 bill. dkk, almost 12 times
larger than that of the actual development of the cycle superhighway network (102 mill. dkk). other basic reference strategies are
valued between -400 mill. dkk and 300 mill. dkk. although optimized according to the net present values, the optimized solutions
are also highly beneficial according to the benefit–cost ratios, yielding a value of 3.63. this is significantly higher than that of the
actual development (1.19) and the basic reference strategies (0.15–1.60).
in the optimization and evaluation, we solely consider travel time benefits and refrain from other types of benefits such as
health benefits. still, in a long-term investment scenario where budgets can accommodate investing in the full project portfolio,
the optimized solutions render a slightly higher net present value (1.3 bill. dkk) more than 1.6 bill. dkk higher than that of the
best performing basic reference strategy. even when forced to invest in the entire portfolio, the proposed optimization heuristic is
still superior, leading to a net present value exceeding 400 mill. dkk, just above 750 mill. dkk higher than that of the best basic
reference strategy. this shows that our highly scalable optimization framework with linear approximations is capable of providing
substantial benefits, and underlines the importance of optimal prioritization with respect to where and when to invest in proposed
network expansions.

appendix b. societal cost–benefit assessment of transport infrastructure projects
b.1. societal cost–benefit performance indicators
in the following we introduce societal cost–benefit performance indicators commonly used to evaluate transport infrastructure
projects. we consider the accumulated measures across all origins, destinations and types, although for simplicity, it is left out in
the notation below.
b.1.1. net present value (npv)
the npv represents the accumulated and discounted benefits for a given year 𝑡, subtracted the corresponding costs,
𝑁𝑃𝑉 (𝑡) =
𝑡∑

(𝜅𝑗
(𝑇𝑆𝑗 +𝐻𝐵𝑗 +𝐴𝐸𝑗 −𝐶𝐶𝑗 −𝑀𝐶𝑗
) +𝑆𝑉𝑗
), 𝑡 ∈ {1, 2, …,|t|}. (b.1)
although defined for every year in the evaluation period t = {1, 2, …,|t|}, it normally refers to the specific value in the final
year of the evaluation period 𝑡 = |t|.
above, 𝑇𝑆𝑗 is the total travel time savings converted to monetary units for year 𝑗, i.e. the sum of user benefits (eq. (b.5))
across all zones and type of cyclists, and is the most commonly included factor in transport appraisal (vickerman, 2007). for all
results reported in the paper, such user benefits are re-calculated using updated cost matrices corresponding to the actual invested
segments for any given year. these effects are included in the net present value calculations due to their common appearance in
transport appraisal (vickerman, 2007), and their relatively large contribution to the overall net present value in recent evaluations
of bicycle infrastructure (standen et al., 2019; rich et al., 2021; fosgerau et al., 2023), as already mentioned in the literature review
(section 2).
𝐻𝐵𝑗 represents the net health benefits gained in year 𝑗. this is a fixed benefit per kilometer: every cycled kilometer render
improved health benefits – and thereby reduced societal health care costs and – but also more kilometers which increase the risk of
a crash. the net health benefits factor applied in this study from technical university of denmark (2020) also takes into account
increase in productivity (flachs et al., 2015). in this paper, we combine these effects into a single net health benefit measure, but
keep the notation 𝐻𝐵𝑗 as the health effects largely dominate the accident costs according to e.g. de hartog et al. (2010) as well as
the official danish unit prices (technical university of denmark, 2020). health benefits are the most commonly included factor in
bicycle infrastructure appraisal (brown et al., 2016), and are also the main contributor to the net present value in e.g. sælensminde
(2004), chapman et al. (2018), rich et al. (2021). because it is driven by demand effects, which is left out of the optimization
problem, the net health benefits are only considered in problem 3.1.2, and are not included in the case study where demand is
assumed constant.
𝐴𝐸𝑗 represents additional external costs that may be included. the literature review (section 2) lists numerous other factors, such
as productivity, congestion, comfort/security, vehicle operating costs, and parking (brown et al., 2016). three of these (congestion,
vehicle operating costs, and parking) require explicit modeling of car traffic. as this study primarily considers upgrade of existing
bicycle infrastructure or new separated cycleways in rural areas, the implications for car traffic is considered to be minimal.
furthermore, as the final model does not take demand effects into account, we cannot incorporate indirect second order effects
from demand changes. likewise, changes in productivity outside those accounted for in the value-of-time and health benefits are
left out as well. finally, although included in some cost–benefit studies (e.g. sælensminde, 2004), the effects on comfort/security
are generally difficult to model and valuate, which is why these are not included in our study. although our model does not take
any of these additional externalities into account, some of them are discussed in section 5.1 regarding future research.
𝐶𝐶𝑗 represent the construction costs for segments built in year 𝑗, whereas 𝑀𝐶𝑗 are the maintenance costs for segments
constructed prior to year 𝑗. the discount factor 𝜅𝑡 discounts the values and is defined as,

b.1.2. benefit–cost ratio (bcr)
this paper also refers to the bcr in a given year 𝑡, defined as:

again, unless explicitly specified otherwise the term refers to the specific value at the final year of the evaluation period 𝑡 = |t|.
notice that, due to the strong connection between construction costs and the scrap value, we have placed the scrap value in the
denominator. it means that from a technical financial perspective, the scrap value is interpreted as a reduction of costs rather than
as a benefit.
both the net present value and the benefit–cost ratio increases when the benefits increases, and decreases when the costs increase.
as such, they measure more or less the same kind of performance, but is interpreted in two different ways (dasgupta and pearce,
1972; leleur, 2000).
b.2. consumer surplus calculation
a conventional cost–benefit analysis typically includes the measurements of direct consumer surplus 𝐶𝑆𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 for zones 𝑖 and 𝑗
for a traveler type 𝑘 through a rule-of-half matrix approximation of travel-time savings (kidokoro, 2004). in the current situation,
however, we assume that the demand is fixed, 3 why the calculation of user benefits becomes slightly simpler. the change in user
benefit 𝑈𝐵𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 is calculated as the change in consumer surplus 𝛥𝐶𝑆𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 for trips between origin 𝑖 and destination 𝑗 by travelers of
type 𝑘. in this paper, 𝑘 represents a combination of different bicycle technologies (conventional bike, e-bike, speed pedelec) and
speed preferences (slow, medium, fast) as described in hallberg et al. (2021).
𝑈𝐵𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝐮𝑡−1) = 𝛥𝐶𝑆𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝐮𝑡−1) = 𝑥0
𝑖,𝑗,𝑘
(

𝑃 0

in eq. (b.5) the probabilities of traveler type 𝑘 using link𝑙 when traveling between𝑖 and𝑗 are denoted by𝑃 0
𝑙,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 in the baseline,
and by 𝑃𝑙,𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 (𝐮𝑡−1) after upgrading the links where 𝐮𝑡−1 is equal to 1. 4 𝑝𝑙,𝑘 represents the link costs of the baseline scenario, while
̂ 𝑝𝑙,𝑘 represents the link costs for upgraded links. these link costs are in line with hallberg et al. (2021) based directly on the travel
times of the individual links,
𝑝𝑙,𝑘 =𝑉𝑜𝑇 ⋅𝜏𝑙,𝑘, ∀𝑙 ∈ n, ∀𝑘 ∈ k, (b.6)
̂ 𝑝𝑙,𝑘 =𝑉𝑜𝑇 ⋅ ̂ 𝜏𝑙,𝑘, ∀𝑙 ∈ n, ∀𝑘 ∈ k. (b.7)
here𝜏𝑙,𝑘 denotes the travel time of link𝑙 for traveler type𝑘, whereas ̂ 𝜏𝑙,𝑘 are the corresponding upgraded travel times. the travel
time of link 𝑙 for type 𝑘, involves in both cases the direct travel time (dependent on type 𝑘) as well as waiting time at intersections
(independent on 𝑘) and is denoted by 𝜏𝑙𝑘, whereas 𝑉𝑜𝑇 denotes the societal value-of-time that transforms travel time savings into
monetary units (91 dkk per hour for cyclists 5).
we note that it is straightforward to consider alternative or additional attributes than travel time as long as they are flow-
independent. in such case, the original and upgraded generalized costs on each link are used instead of travel time. fosgerau et al.
(2023) exemplifies how such generalized cost can be converted into monetary terms. the inclusion of additional link attributes are
also considered in section 5.1 on future research.

4 in this study, as in hallberg et al. (2021), the flow is assigned entirely to the (𝑖,𝑗,𝑘 )-specific shortest paths. the methodology easily extends to situations
with more than a single path per od, in which case the travel times are weighted by the corresponding choice probabilities.
